#252. The Terrifying True Story of Witchcraft and Murder in Swipe Right on the Devil

Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

Sydney Loofe, whose murder is explored in Swipe Right on the Devil

Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts.

Update your preferences in Account Settings

Nebraska native Sydney Loofe was looking for love, or at least for some fun, while scrolling through Tinder back in 2017. But the resulting date quickly went wrong, and she never made it back home. The new three-part Hulu true crime docuseries Swipe Right on the Devil tells the harrowing tale behind Loofe’s 2017 murder, how she was lured into a satanic BDSM ritual for so-called witchcraft, and how her murder led to authorities uncovering a years-long scheme run by two cult leaders. It tells the story, in part, through interviews with surviving women who escaped the cult weeks prior to her murder.

The docuseries marks the first time the Loofe clan is sharing their side of the story, along with Nebraska investigators and an FBI agent assigned to the case. Loofe’s mother Susie Loofe wrote on Facebook that her family had been contacted by at least 100 production companies looking to make a documentary about the murder before putting their trust in Imagine Documentaries.

Swipe Right on the Devil director and executive producer Brian Biegel tells TIME that Loofe’s “mysterious disappearance” quickly became a far more “complex [story] than the headlines suggested.” Biegel, who previously worked with FBI officers while helming the ESPN 30for30 documentary The Counterfeiter, spent approximately three years immersing himself in the Loofe case. The experience forever changed his views on the occult: “As a documentary filmmaker, I'm naturally skeptical and always looking for evidence, but I also believe there are aspects of the human experience we don't fully understand,” he says. He spent years talking with the man convicted of Loofe’s murder and hearing firsthand from the women he drew into his orbit. “I wouldn't say this experience made me a believer in the supernatural, but it did make me more open-minded about what might exist beyond the physical world.”

A Facebook post from the period when Loofe was missing Courtesy of Hulu

Loofe, a 24-year-old hardware store clerk at Menard's in Lincoln, Neb., first connected on Tinder with a woman she believed to be named Audrey. Loofe went on two dates with “Audrey.” But she never came home from the second one, on Nov. 15, 2017. When she didn’t show up for work the next day, Loofe’s coworkers called the police to do a wellness check at her home; the officers found her car in the driveway and her purse still in her apartment. Loofe was reported missing.

It was then that one of Loofe’s friends took matters into their own hands: They created a fake Tinder profile to find Audrey, who seemed to be the last person Loofe had made contact with. Loofe’s friend matched with Audrey and gave her details to investigators, who soon discovered that the phone number belonged to a 23-year-old woman named Bailey Boswell. The search for Loofe continued.

Her family held out hope that she would be found unharmed. But their prayers went unanswered. Loofe’s remains were found in garbage bags on Dec. 4 along the state highway in rural Clay County, Neb. She had been dismembered into 14 pieces, in what seemed to be a ritualistic manner. Her left upper arm was never recovered, and her internal organs were still missing. Authorities then set out to find out what really happened to the woman her family described as “the crystal-blue-eyed girl with a soft heart”—and why.

Aubrey Trail and Bailey Boswell Courtesy of Hulu

Boswell’s social media apps were just the beginning of the disturbing investigation. Boswell turned out to be part of a couple with Aubrey Trail, 51, a self-declared vampire. The duo shared an apartment and both had extensive criminal histories, including theft and financial scams, across various states. Loofe was just one of the many targets that both Boswell and Trail had found on Tinder to try to lure into their makeshift “coven” under a cult-like manifesto.

A trio of women in their early 20s,  all of whom have chosen to remain anonymous, testified in court and later in Swipe Right on the Devil that they had each met Boswell on Tinder, and were soon also introduced to Trail. They were showered with expensive clothes, spa treatments, and drugs, and given weekly allowances ranging from $150 to $200. The cost? Participating in group sex with Trail (“Daddy”) and Boswell (“Mommy”), while also helping the couple sell stolen goods. The women each testified that Trail would call himself a “vampire,” and claim that he had supernatural powers such as being able to fly and read minds. Trail also told the women that they were all now “witches” belonging to his cult, with Boswell as their leader.

Both Trail and Boswell claimed they were practicing witchcraft and black magic with required ritualistic violent acts to gain entry to their coven. Trail told the members of the “coven” that they would have to torture someone and complete a killing in order for him to gain more power as their messiah; he also wanted to film the murder and share the profits of the snuff film with each of them. The women claimed they left Trail’s cult shortly thereafter in November 2017, right before Boswell lured in Loofe.

After authorities discovered that Trail was connected to Boswell, they searched the couple’s home and found a BDSM dog leash, duct tape, “a sauna suit with the crotch cut out, zip ties, a hatchet, a plastic drop cloth smeared with blood, and a book on anatomy.”

In the trailer for Swipe Right on the Devil, one of the women claims that Trail told her after meeting on Tinder that he and Boswell “knew witchcraft and had 12 women [and] needed a thirteenth to complete the coven.” Another survivor describes how Trail had “rules” for the coven which included being naked while around him and allowing him to watch lesbian encounters. The documentary features a segment in which one of the women tells an officer that to be in the “the inner circle,” he said she would “have had to kill somebody and take their soul.”

“While this is fundamentally Sydney's story, it's also about the other young women manipulated by Aubrey Trail and Bailey Boswell at a vulnerable time in their lives, when they were searching for something universal: love and connection,” says Biegel. “They spoke candidly about the challenges of finding queer relationships in their conservative Midwestern communities, and how Bailey exploited that loneliness through Tinder. Once drawn into their world, they found themselves trapped in a disturbing web of manipulation, witchcraft, and demands to kill as a path to supernatural power.”

Biegel’s fellow executive producer and Imagine Documentaries executive Christopher St. John adds that the team behind docuseries was “struck by the bravery of the three women who tell their stories at the center of this series.” St. John continues, “They lived through such an incredibly traumatic experience, but they still believe it’s valuable to share the story, whether in court or on camera.” He says the team elected to tell the story in three episodes because they had too much material to do the story justice in a feature-length film, and even still, there were elements that didn’t make it into the final cut. “Hopefully, viewers will really get an accurate sense of their thinking and where they were during this intense period of their lives.”

Aubrey Trail during questioning Courtesy of Hulu

Trail confessed to Loofe’s killing right away, but maintained that her death was an accident. He told the police that he had engaged in consensual BDSM acts with Loofe and two other women, with the participants paying him $15,000 to film. Trail said that he had strangled Loofe with an extension cord during an erotic act and did not intend to kill her. Following her death, Trail dismembered Loofe’s body to avoid getting caught. He emphasized that Boswell was not part of the crime at all. During messages uncovered throughout the investigations, Trail is shown telling Boswell that he should be the one taking all the blame, and that she should change her narrative to come across as another “victim.”

During the three-week trial, Trail yelled out in court that Boswell was “innocent” and then slit his throat with a shank; the trial was put on hold as he was rushed to the hospital and recovered. Trail also suffered from two heart attacks and a stroke over the course of the case being presented to a jury. He changed his story during the trial, telling the jury that he killed Loofe out of fear that she would “expose my lifestyle.”

Trail was found guilty on all counts of first-degree murder, conspiracy, and improper disposal of human remains. He was sentenced to death on June 9, 2021. Trail currently is on death row and petitioned the state of Nebraska in August 2023 to set his execution date; Trail is 10th in line on death row for the state. The first and only execution via lethal injection in Nebraska took place in 2018, with the execution of convicted murderer Carey Dean Moore. Trail’s appeal in 2025 was denied.

It was during Trail’s time on death row that he started to open up to Swipe Right on the Devil filmmaker Biegel. Over the course of a year, Biegel sought to gain Trail’s trust and participation. “Ultimately, that persistence gave us something rare in true crime: 360-degree access to the story, allowing us to explore its many layers and conflicting perspectives,” explains Biegel.

Boswell stood trial in September 2020. There was ample physical evidence for the case against her, including DNA found on a latex glove that was in the same trash bag as Loofe’s remains. Boswell was found guilty on all counts of first-degree murder, conspiracy, and improper disposal of human remains in October 2020, and was sentenced to life imprisonment in November 2021.

“I don't see Swipe Right on the Devil as another true-crime retelling. It's a discovery of a mystery, unfolding for the first time through the firsthand accounts of those who lived it,” says Biegel. “This isn't a whodunnit: It's a whydunnit. What really happened to Sydney Loofe, and why? The series explores the unsettling collision of ancient witchcraft beliefs and modern digital dating culture. [These are] two killers who, by their own accounts, would have continued killing had they not been caught so soon.”

Sydney Loofe’s family launched the Sydney Loofe Foundation in 2019. The foundation has raised more than $30,000 to be awarded in scholarships and $26,000 to be distributed to community causes. “Sydney, with a ‘y,’ never stopped to ask ‘why’ when it came to helping others,” the Loofe family wrote on the foundation website. “Although she often struggled physically, financially, and emotionally, she opened her home and heart to others. And those who knew Sydney knew of her love of family, her affinity for animals and pets (especially cats), her interest in fishing as a pastime, her fascination with beautiful crystals, and her soft heart…Sydney Loofe was a unique child of God, one who proudly had this tattooed on her arm, ‘Everything will be wonderful someday.’ Her family holds tight to the faith that indeed in heaven, that is true.”

This story originally misstated the month in which Bailey Boswell was sentenced to life in prison. It was November 2021, not July 2021.

#253. Colorado Marijuana Regulators Failed to Protect Consumers From Potential Contamination, Audit Finds

Investigative Journalism in the Public Interest

Investigative Journalism in the Public Interest

Investigative Journalism in the Public Interest

ProPublica is a nonprofit, investigative newsroom that exposes corruption. We report in all 50 states and partner with local newsrooms. Our work spurs real-world impact and has received numerous awards, including nine Pulitzer Prizes.

How Abortion Bans Lead to Preventable Deaths

How Recent Arrivals at the Border Have Changed the Country and Its Attitudes

SCOTUS Justices’ Beneficial Relationships With Billionaire Donors

This article was produced for ProPublica’s Local Reporting Network in partnership with The Denver Gazette. Sign up for Dispatches to get our stories in your inbox every week.

The agency charged with regulating Colorado’s first-in-the-nation legal recreational marijuana market potentially put consumers at risk of using contaminated products, a new state audit found.

The report pointed to inconsistent oversight by the Marijuana Enforcement Division and loose state rules around testing marijuana products or contaminants. As a result, it found, nearly 75% of marijuana processed for eventual sale from 2023 through 2025 evaded testing for pesticides.

The audit, released Monday, identified a host of issues, including regulators’ failure to meet regular inspection schedules or to conduct sufficient random checks at dispensaries for contaminated products. It also found that delayed agency warnings to the public allowed potentially contaminated products to remain on shelves for months, sometimes years.

The audit’s conclusions echo several findings of an investigation this year by The Denver Gazette and ProPublica into the Marijuana Enforcement Division’s lapses in policing contaminants entering the legal marketplace.

In one case highlighted by the newsrooms, it took 15 months for a manufacturer to discontinue production of a marijuana sleep aid pill despite consumers warning regulators that the product was harming their livers. The pills had herbal extracts that researchers say can cause liver damage.

Auditors said they did not identify evidence of “widespread health and safety issues” but did find that deficient regulations still left consumers vulnerable.

The audit found that unsafe products had remained on the market for as long as three years before the Marijuana Enforcement Division issued a public warning. The auditor’s review of the agency’s health and safety advisories found that, on average, from 2023 through 2025, contaminated products that were the subject of an advisory remained on shelves for more than seven months. That’s the same number of months the news organizations found contaminated products stayed on shelves before warnings were issued.

The audit also found that it took the agency three and a half months, on average, in 2025 to issue an advisory once it launched an investigation into potential contamination. Even after investigators determined an advisory was needed, it still took the agency a month on average to issue one.

“These issues limit MED’s ability to meet its core objective to identify product safety risks and to help ensure that licensed marijuana businesses meet product safety requirements,” the audit stated. “As a result, there is an increased risk that marijuana products sold in Colorado may have contaminants or that the potency listed on the labels is inaccurate.”

We reported that a top Marijuana Enforcement Division official acknowledged in a meeting with industry representatives that the amount of chemically converted hemp being sold as marijuana is greater than the agency has publicly disclosed.

State regulators announced a plan to crack down on companies that illegally sell cheaper and potentially hazardous hemp products as marijuana, citing potential tax avoidance and “serious risks to public safety.”

Colorado regulators discussed a proposal to do away with the current testing system that allows marijuana manufacturers to choose their own samples. Instead, independent labs or contractors would collect the samples.

The Denver Gazette and ProPublica reported that intoxicating hemp has seeped into Colorado’s marijuana market because the state had failed to adopt safety measures. Tests found signs of hemp in vapes sold at marijuana dispensaries.

After hemp companies figured out how to convert CBD into the high-inducing compound THC, Colorado became one of the first states to ban chemically converted hemp products, first with regulatory advisories and then with legislation.

President Donald Trump signed the Farm Bill, which legalizes hemp. Congress’ intent was to help farmers along with people who believe hemp’s high levels of the nonintoxicating compound CBD can help with seizures, pain and sleep.

Colorado became the first state to allow retail sales of marijuana for recreational use.

Colorado allows marijuana businesses to test far less frequently than required by state guidelines if they can show consistent compliance with testing rules. The reduced testing program results “in a significant portion of the marijuana product being sold in Colorado not undergoing testing to ensure it is safe,” the audit stated. Manufacturers also can rely on other testing exemptions, such as for certain marijuana-derived skin and body products, but there is “limited MED oversight” of how manufacturers determine they qualify for an exemption.

Auditors reported that a survey of MED investigators found that more than half said there are or may be widespread marijuana product safety issues in Colorado’s regulated market.

In a statement released Monday night, the Marijuana Enforcement Division emphasized that state auditors found no evidence “to support claims of widespread health and safety issues” with marijuana products for sale in Colorado, but the agency agreed the audit identified areas for improvement. It said that among other things, it would be assessing exemptions to tests currently allowed under state laws and regulations.

The state auditor’s review “allows us to take a critical look at how we can continue to carry out our work, consistent with our commitment to integrity, safety, and continuous operational improvement,” Dominique Mendiola, senior director of the MED, said in a statement.

The auditor’s report highlighted a criticism her agency has repeatedly sidestepped: the absence of independent collection of samples for safety testing. The Denver Gazette and ProPublica found that more than half of states with regulated marijuana markets rely on private labs or an independent third party to collect samples. Colorado marijuana businesses are free to collect their own samples submitted for testing.

The enforcement division’s own investigators told auditors that, as a result, Colorado relies largely on an honor code to ensure the safety of marijuana sold in the state’s dispensaries.

Auditors said that allowing businesses to select their own samples “increases the risk that businesses do not pick samples that are representative of the overall quality of their marijuana product.”

MED regulators at the start of the year said they planned to pursue an overhaul that would have required private lab personnel to collect samples, but they abandoned that effort after manufacturers said it would increase their costs. On Monday, Kyle Lambert, the enforcement division’s deputy senior director, told legislators that the agency opted against an overhaul this year, chiefly because he said it would be a geographic challenge to do away with self-sampling for rural businesses.

“It was decided now wasn’t the right time to propose that as a policy change,” Lambert said, adding that the agency still might revisit the issue.

State Sen. Byron Pelton, a Republican from Sterling, pushed back, saying it was a loophole he thinks the agency should consider closing.

“If I’m a shady character, I know which products I’m going to go sampling and which ones I’m not,” he said. “That’s a pretty gray area to me.”

The audit also questioned the agency’s reliance on businesses voluntarily recalling contaminated marijuana products, saying that “some of these recalls did not succeed at stopping the businesses from selling potentially unsafe product.”

Just last month, The Denver Gazette and ProPublica reported that a Colorado manufacturer voluntarily flagged 49 of its popular marijuana vapes for recall, nearly six weeks after a third-party lab notified the company and regulators that it detected methylene chloride, a chemical the Environmental Protection Agency has sought to ban for most uses because it can cause cancer. The MED issued a health and safety advisory a day after the publications first reported the manufacturer had warned dispensaries to stop selling the vapes, which consumers had been purchasing for over a year.

#254. Coming soon to L.A.: Your new favorite bookshop is a mystical cocktail bar with a dragon

This is read by an automated voice. Please report any issues or inconsistencies here.

See more from the L.A. Times in Google Search. Set us as preferred

Genres Book Society, a new bookshop coming to L.A., is hiding a few secrets. The first indication that something a bit fantastical is going on here is the fact that the boutique store has a mascot.

Look up upon entering the space, and you’ll meet Lore. Lore is a dragon, and this dragon is made of books. The striking sculpture will hover over a cozy space with modular bookshelves that are arranged on a floor covered in book quotes. Peer closely and you’ll see the quotes are designed in the shape of a tree.

A story is being built, a narrative that signifies that Genres aims to be not just a bookshop, but a place that feels like a real-life fairy tale. For this bookstore is also concealing a speakeasy of sorts, a secluded cafe and bar with multiple themed spaces and a two-story tree with glowing books growing out of its branches.

“This is for people who want to enter into story worlds,” says Ann Morrow Johnson of design firm Gensler. Johnson, a former creative with Walt Disney Imagineering, leads Gensler’s entertainment and experiences division, which is designing the bookshop.

She wants guests to feel transported into a fantasy universe, and understands how to make that happen. For instance? “Adding just a little bit of theatricality and sparkle helps create an invitation of suspension of disbelief,” she says.

Genres, on track to open in mid-2027 near the intersection of Melrose and Fairfax avenues, is tapping into a local trend of heavily designed thematic spaces with a penchant for fantasy. Think, for instance, of the Witch’s Cottage in North Hollywood, or the Last Canteen in the shadow of Universal Studios Hollywood. Consider it the latest evolution of the themed restaurant, and a way to bring the heavily theatrical worlds of theme parks closer to home. Johnson, for instance, says the firm’s research with the the Immersive Experience Institute has shown a hunger in younger generations to embrace spaces such as the Rainforest Cafe.

“People are seeking out a moment,” Johnson says. “Those more theatrical, highly scenic spaces are more likely to get you off your phone. Little moments of delight center you.”

Whether it’s AI, corporate consolidation, inflation or the struggles of Hollywood that’s got you down, the Last Canteen near Universal Studios wants to be your “Cheers.”

Genres was dreamed up Clare Grant and Karissa Marston, who brought the project to Gensler and tapped them to lead its design. Grant, an actor/producer, says the idea came to her while experiencing the regret of missing a retreat hosted by her girlfriends, who had rented a cabin to read the latest edition in the fantasy “Crescent City” series.

“Just having a newborn, I was like, ‘Why can’t I go read this book in a fantasy-themed cabin?’ That’s literally my dream,” Grant says. “Then I started thinking about it. Why don’t I have something like this in Los Angeles, where I can pop in for an afternoon or evening where someone is handing me drinks and food in an immersive space?”

An insider guide to the ever-changing world of theme parks, coming to you straight from SoCal — the theme park capital of the world.

Marston, it turns out, who has experience in hospitality and marketing with an emphasis on fandom, was already dreaming of such a space. The two of them crafted a business plan for a bookstore, one that wouldn’t look out of place in a sword and sorcery fantasy novel, with a hidden speakeasy.

“I was in Paris, and I went to the Shakespeare bookstore, and it was truly the most magical experience I have ever had,” Marston says. “I walked in and felt so immersed in the books and the history and I went up to the second floor and sat up there and wept. I was like, ‘I never want to leave this place. I only ever want to feel this way and be surrounded by books and people who love books.’”

Come to the Witch’s Cottage in NoHo for the hidden wonders, but stay for dragon-themed deviled eggs and rum-heavy cocktails.

Their vision is grand. The bookstore, they hope, will become a gathering spot for authors in the fantasy and sci-fi realms. And they talk of the speakeasy as not just being a safe eatery to sit and read all day, but also a future venue for, say, immersive theater. The two-story space will house multiple themed rooms, either for hanging out and hiding, or for potential events.

“I think it’s safe to say that if you wanted to be transported to outer space, you would be pleasantly surprised,” Marston says. “Or if you wanted to escape to Dracula’s castle or some of these other really big genres that you have, readers will be pleasantly surprised at the worlds that we are building.”

Johnson says Genres will be flexible enough to welcome introverts who want to dream away the day with a book, but can also be a home for a baby shower in addition to an author signing or a theatrical event. That’s in part why the shop and ground floor of the speakeasy have a familiar fantasy vibe, one not out of place in, say, the fairy tales of the Brothers Grimm, albeit with the welcoming tone of Disney.

“The focal point of the space is this sort of epic woodland fantasy,” Marston says. “When we first brought this to Gensler, and showed them our inspo deck, we kept saying ‘A Midsummer Night’s Dream.’”

That’s key, as everything in the space will spring from the written word. Genres, in that sense, also feels refreshing, emphasizing actual books and tactile media at a cultural moment when AI is often dominating conversations. Grant and Marston speak of being influenced by passionate social media creators who celebrate books, wanting to build a community as much as an event space. A prerequisite to be a bartender, says Marston, will need to be a love of books.

“We’re calling all our bartenders book-tenders,” Marston says. “Not only will they be well-versed in all things spirits and cocktails, but they are also someone who can give you the next book recommendation. These are very specialized employees. They can curate cocktails and books for you.”

Consider Genres a place for dinner and drinks, but one where story is always on the menu.

“People are craving more than dinner,” Grant says. “They want experience. They want magical memories that can get them through harder moments because nothing is easy right now. People want to spend money on something that is more than just dinner or going to a bar. Like whimsy.”

The essential weekly guide to enjoying the outdoors in Southern California. Insider tips on the best of our beaches, trails, parks, deserts, forests and mountains.

Todd Martens is a features columnist at the Los Angeles Times who writes about theme parks and West Coast Experiences, among other topics. His weekly newsletter, Mr. Todd’s Wild Ride, covers the ever-changing world of theme parks from SoCal. Martens joined the Los Angeles Times in 2007 and has covered a mix of interactive entertainment as its game critic and pop music as a reporter and editor. Previously, he reported on the music business for Billboard Magazine.

#255. Democrats accuse Trump of using prosecution threats to pressure election officials

Democratic groups sued the Trump administration Tuesday, accusing it of using unfounded voter fraud claims to muscle its way into how states run elections.

Why it matters: The groups say the administration is issuing a stream of threats to intimidate and bully both voters and election officials.

  • They're asking a judge to declare the administration's actions unlawful and block further interference.

Driving the news: The lawsuit condemns the administration for targeting state voter rolls by insisting that local officials review federally compiled lists of alleged noncitizens on their voter rolls.

  • The suit also alleges that the administration is improperly demanding that states deny ballots to registered voters based on those federal lists.
  • The Democrat-aligned groups also claim the administration has attempted to intimidate state and local officials by threatening them with jail time or prosecution.

Case in point: The lawsuit cites a Department of Homeland Security claim that Nevada had nearly 16,000 noncitizens on its voter rolls.

  • The department later claimed that it had confirmed 185 of those people were noncitizens. State records later indicated that all 185 were citizens.
  • The DOJ also demanded that California deny ballots to more than 20,000 people based on a federal list that flagged them as suspected ineligible voters, the lawsuit says.
  • A preliminary investigation by Los Angeles County investigators found that more than 25% of those flagged listed a U.S. birthplace, and others already had their voter registrations canceled.

What they're saying: "Department of Justice and DHS officials have sought to bolster their interference efforts through a persistent stream of threats and intimidation directed towards state and local election officials across the country," the groups' attorneys wrote.

  • "Defendants' coercive threats of legal retaliation against state officials, including even potential imprisonment, for simply following state law and aiding registered voters in casting their ballots objectively intimidate voters and election officials alike, in violation of Section 11(b) of the Voting Rights Act."
  • The suit, brought by Democratic organizations including the Democratic Congressional Campaign Committee, Democratic leaders and a voter, names Trump, the Departments of Justice and Homeland Security, and their respective heads as defendants.

The other side: "The Justice Department enforces federal election law, and election integrity is a top priority," a DOJ spokesperson told Axios.

  • "Every action the Department has taken to protect the integrity of this year's elections is grounded in the law and being litigated in open court. Ensuring that only eligible citizens vote and that every lawful ballot is counted is a requirement of federal law," according to the emailed statement.
  • "We notified California that noncitizens are likely registered to vote, and we asked the state to independently verify the citizenship of any ballots cast by people on a list that we provided. It refused to do so. Federal law doesn't permit this willful blindness."

A White House spokesperson told Axios that while "Democrats and the media continue to dismiss concerns about voter fraud, the Trump Administration is taking decisive lawful action to protect our elections including prosecuting election crimes, strengthening election infrastructure security, and protecting vulnerable voters from fraud and coercion."

  • A spokesperson for Homeland Security told Axios that the department's "message to aliens who vote in American elections is clear: we will find you, arrest you, and you will face the consequences — including criminal charges and deportation."

Zoom out: The suit also argues that the president has no constitutional authority to decide who gets to vote or dictate how states count ballots.

  • Investigations and audits have found no evidence of widespread voter fraud. Research shows noncitizen voting is rare, and there's no evidence it occurs on a scale that could swing elections.

Go deeper: Trump sues California to prevent counting 20,000 voters' ballots

Editor's note: This story has been updated with comment from the White House and DHS.

#256. Mourinho keeps Clasico midas touch 13 years on as La Liga heats up

Real Madrid have now won five consecutive games in all competitions

The Real Madrid manager celebrated Kylian Mbappe's late winner at Barcelona on Sunday with a trademark knee slide, as he maintained his midas touch over their bitter rivals.

Mourinho has become just the third Real boss to go six El Clasicos in a row without defeat - 13 years after he last led Los Blancos in this fixture.

Four wins and two draws in his past six matches in charge of Real Madrid against Barcelona put him up there with Miguel Munoz (1962 to 1965) and Vicente del Bosque (2001 to 2003) as Real managers who have gone six in a row without defeat against the old enemy.

It was also just a second victory in eight games against Barcelona for Real, with the return of Mourinho to the fixture haunting the reigning champions

And more importantly for Mourinho, Mbappe's double in Real's 2-1 win ended Barcelona's nine-game unbeaten domestic run with a crucial Clasico victory at the Camp Nou.

A Real defeat would have allowed Barcelona to establish a commanding nine-point advantage but the result has transformed the top of La Liga, bringing Mourinho's side to within three points of their rivals.

"In my view, Real Madrid do not play to win at the Camp Nou, Real Madrid play to win La Liga," he said.

"We could win with luck, but we won with calmness and with all merit," he said. "It's a very strong group. Everyone works very hard. Everyone was ready to win.

"The credit goes to the players. When you win, the credit goes to the players. But when you lose, the blame falls on you. And that's why we see coaches being sacked from their positions every week."

The Portuguese, who returned to Real 13 years after his first stint in charge this summer, has now won six and lost six El Clasicos.

He said: "I've learned that I have an extraordinary group, with the quality and mindset to play.

"In midfield, having a mix of talents gives us more than just having two players with similar characteristics. This mix of talents gives us balance when defending and quality when we go on the attack.

"We've beaten several teams by four or more goals, but this is Barca, and the players put on a great performance. We need to continue like this and don't go over the moon after this win."

Barcelona boss Hansi Flick said he didn't want to give excuses for the defeat but admitted his team lacked fresh legs after a demanding few weeks.

"We missed the freshness," he said. "It's not easy for our players. We have to recover well, train well and we have to respond from this defeat.

"In big games, small things are important and they decide the games and today, it wasn't on our side.

"I can see that today we were not fresh and this is what we have to change."

Mbappe double fires Real Madrid to win at leaders Barcelona

#257. What do you do when your love and your life are in two different places?

When news breaks, you need to understand what actually matters — and what to do about it. At Vox, our mission to help you make sense of the world has never been more vital. But we can’t do it on our own.

We rely on readers like you to fund our journalism. Will you support our work and become a Vox Member today?

How to make a huge decision when there’s no perfect choice.﻿

Hi readers! Shayla Love here, science journalist and longtime fan of Your Mileage May Vary. I’m honored to be subbing for Sigal Samuel while she’s out on parental leave. I’m diving into your questions as a way to help understand human nature and our choices through multiple lenses: philosophical, psychological, and beyond. Please send in any emotional, body/brain, sociological, perceptual, or other kind of life quandaries you might have.

I am someone who notoriously struggles with big decisions. Over a year ago, I found a wonderful partner, who very much feels like they could be my soulmate. We’ve been dating three time zones apart for a while. Being near our families, communities, and aging parents is a shared value — unfortunately one that makes it hard to compromise on location. Either way, I have to give up something I want and value — a wonderful partner who I can see a life with (the first time I’ve felt this way about someone), or the place I’ve always imagined settling down and that represents the person I am becoming and values I want to live. Two good things are pulling me toward opposite paths and there’s not much room for compromise. How do I make the “right” decision?

You’ve found a place in the world that you feel connected to and you met a person you envision spending the rest of your life with. Geographically restless and lonely people everywhere are jealous! But with your good fortunes seemingly at odds with one another, it feels that by choosing one, you’ll forsake the other — and doom the choice you did make to feel like a sacrifice.

When circumstances have forced me to make a tough decision, I’ve daydreamed about a thought experiment: the Many Worlds Interpretation.

In quantum physics, particles behave differently than us mortals. For example, an electron can be in more than one place at once until it’s measured. How can it be in multiple locations and then be observed to be in a single position? In the 1950s, the American physicist Hugh Everett proposed an explanation: The universe splits into alternate versions of itself, one for each place a particle could be measured. To apply this theory to your life, if you chose to move cities, don’t worry, another universe would be created in which you didn’t choose to do this, and you’ll stay right where you are!

Yes, I know you can’t transport yourself into such a quantum realm, but there is something helpful in thinking about alternative universes. Did you have an immediate reaction to which universe you would be upset not to inhabit? Take that information to heart, because it’s likely you’re not ultimately going to make your choice based on a tidy pro-con list.

The psychologists Daniel Kahneman and Amos Tversky won a Nobel Prize for pointing out that most of our decisions are not based on rationality. People use two systems for decision-making: System one is faster and emotionally based, and system two is more logical and slow. But even the slower, more deliberate decision-making can fall prey to any number of cognitive biases.

In your case, one called loss aversion may be especially relevant. People have the tendency to worry more about what they’ll lose, even if what they stand to gain is equal in value. For example, it will likely feel worse to lose a $10 bill from your pocket than it would feel good to find a $10 bill on the ground. This bias is relevant in people’s social lives too: They hold onto older, maybe fading, friendships because the thought of losing them is too painful, but don’t seek out new friends with equal effort. Or, they stay in a meh job because it feels scary to let it go, rather than applying that same energy to finding a new one. Once you accept that your decision-making process will be subject to messy feelings and biases, you can let go of trying to make the perfect choice.

You opened your question by sharing that you struggle with big decisions, and it makes me wonder if you simply feel anxiety about making a choice at all. This is called analysis paralysis. When outcomes are uncertain, anxiety wakes up, and a voice in your head tells you to avoid taking risks. People with analysis paralysis overthink their options, over-research, and second-guess their first instincts. These actions have more to do with stress over committing to any decision rather than the content of what you’re deciding on.

A surprising finding from psychology may ease your paralysis. Researchers told people to make a big life decision — on the order of ending a relationship or quitting their job — by flipping a coin. Heads, they made the change; tails, they didn’t. For those who followed the results of the toss, the people who made the change were overwhelmingly happier, even though their decision to do so was made by pure chance. These random-flip outcomes also show “that people may be excessively cautious when facing life-changing choices,” the authors wrote. The coin helped the participants resist the bias of tending to stick with the status quo, even if it doesn’t make them happier.

To begin to loosen your paralysis, something that might assist you, paradoxically, is to introduce more options into the mix. Jacques Derrida, the 20th-century French philosopher, is known for the concept of deconstruction, a way of analyzing texts or ideas more critically, and showing they’re not as stable as they seem. He pointed out that we often think in binaries and false opposites: good vs. evil, man vs. woman, nature vs. culture. This can set up an easy dichotomy in which one of the items is good, and the other, by being its opposite, is bad.

You’ve created a binary situation, and you don’t actually have to be in one. What if there’s a third option, or a fourth? What if you and your partner move to a brand-new city, and set out to create new community, together? What if you and your partner find a way to split your time between your respective hometowns? Now you don’t have only two good things pulling you in opposite directions, you would have different options you can be more creative with. You also don’t have to commit to the decision you make now for the rest of your life. You can always move cities again, or try to find another mate.

You are with a partner you love, and live in a place that you love. You might reframe your situation as a win-win, rather than a lose-lose. And remember that if you don’t make a choice, you’re not successfully avoiding a decision — it’s a choice of its own. Your partner may get tired of long-distance or end the relationship for another reason. Or, you could start to resent the place you live since you have to travel so much to spend time with your partner.

I also want to point out how you describe your partner. Don’t turn your nose up at finding someone who you can see making a life with, especially as you note, if you’ve never felt that way before. Billionaire investor Warren Buffett has said that there were two most important moments in his life: when he came out of the womb, and when he met his wife. “What happened with me would not have happened without her.” Buffett’s view is supported by research that shows that people with supportive spouses tend to take on more rewarding challenges throughout their lives, and end up having more personal growth and happiness.

Another quantum idea that might interest you is spooky action at a distance. This is when two particles, even far apart, become connected to one another. Measuring one affects the other, no matter how far apart they are. If your partner feels like your “soulmate,” perhaps you have become entangled already. Don’t fret about making the perfect choice about the next phase of life, but do make a choice (any choice!) that considers your futures as bound up together. For the other details, maybe it’s time to find a coin to flip.

Here at Vox, we're unwavering in our commitment to covering the issues that matter most to you — threats to democracy, immigration, reproductive rights, the environment, and the rising polarization across this country.

Our mission is to provide clear, accessible journalism that empowers you to stay informed and engaged in shaping our world. By becoming a Vox Member, you directly strengthen our ability to deliver in-depth, independent reporting that drives meaningful change.

We accept credit card, Apple Pay, and Google Pay.

If cost is a barrier, we’d still love for you to be a Vox Member. Apply here to receive a free annual Membership, made possible by another reader.

A law professor explains how AI-assisted weapons subvert our justice system.

Our relentless appetite for meat puts animals through grueling journeys.

A little-known system determines what surgeries are worth. It almost always values men more highly.

﻿The SunZia wind farm is a marvel. Why did it take 20 years to build?

﻿No previous Ebola outbreak has grown this quickly.

#258. A Mathematician Reads the AI Future

My field is as much an expression of the human spirit as music and literature. Is it for the machines now?

#259. Maria Bamford Asks for the Check

On a recent Wednesday morning, in a small black-box theatre in East Los Angeles, I watched the comedian Maria Bamford try out new material. There was no alcohol, but there were free doughnuts. Women in athleisure sipped lattes. Someone brought a baby. The show started promptly, at 9 A.M.

Bamford, among the most daring and charismatic performers of her generation, is always testing the boundaries of what counts as standup comedy. Is it still standup if you’re telling jokes to a group of high schoolers in the aisles of an independent bookstore? Or to friends sitting on a park bench? Bamford does both of these things in her 2017 Netflix special, “Old Baby.” In “The Special Special Special,” she performs for the most intimate audience imaginable: her parents. Bamford’s repertoire includes painfully specific impersonations of her mother and father, and it is uncomfortably thrilling to watch them watch her imitate them, in between jokes about depression and suicide.

Mental illness—and the stigma that surrounds it—is a recurring theme in Bamford’s work. Growing up in Duluth, Minnesota, Bamford struggled with bulimia and intrusive thoughts, though it wasn’t until she was in her thirties that she received the clarifying diagnosis of obsessive-compulsive disorder. (One of her jokes turns on the absurdity of talking about physical illnesses the way people often talk about mental-health problems: “I was dating this chick, and three months in, she tells me that she wears glasses. I was, like, listen, I’m not into all that Western medicine shit. If you want to see, then work at it. Figure out how not to be so myopic. You know?”) When Bamford turned forty, she had a hypomanic episode and was diagnosed as having bipolar II; for the next year, she ended up in and out of psychiatric hospitals.

These struggles are recounted in “Paralyzed by Hope: The Maria Bamford Story,” a new documentary directed by Judd Apatow and Neil Berkeley. The filmmakers interview not only Bamford but also her family and admirers in the industry. Stephen Colbert and Patton Oswalt tell the directors that Bamford is their favorite comedian. Ted Sarandos, a co-C.E.O. of Netflix, calls “Lady Dynamite,” a fictional TV show loosely based on Bamford’s life, his favorite Netflix original series.

In the documentary Bamford is living with her husband, the artist Scott Marvel Cassidy, in their home, in the L.A. suburb of Altadena. But not long after filming ended, around the time of the 2025 wildfires, the couple divorced. Earlier this month, I visited Bamford at her new place, a one-bedroom apartment in Highland Park. Bamford has been touring non-stop this fall; when we met, she was taking care of some laundry between trips to Brooklyn and British Columbia. Over the sounds of her dryer, we spoke about participating in documentaries, how much she made from her Netflix special, and how standup can function as a form of therapy. Our conversation has been condensed and edited for clarity.

You started filming the documentary four years ago. How did Judd Apatow first reach out to you with the idea?

He texted the night before, saying, “Hey, can I stop by and start filming?” I was, like, “Sure,” but I was texting my manager, “Hey, Judd Apatow wants me to shoot something. Can you talk to him about money?” My manager—the darling, the sweet, the extremely proficient and professional Bruce Smith—said, “But . . . it’s Judd.” So then I was, like, “O.K., I think I’m on my own here.” I texted Judd back. I think I asked for eight hundred dollars.

He brings you five hundred dollars in cash in the first scene of the film. And then he gives you ten thousand dollars at the very end, right?

Part of it was, they were going to shoot me performing one on one for a fan at a coffee shop, so I wanted to pay the fan. Then, I also wanted to pay the coffee-shop barista who was working that day—a hundred bucks or something. And then, I wanted to get paid.

It’s not really about the money. I guess I need it, but it’s also that I think of it as a job, and I don’t want to be a victim of my job, where it’s, like,  “Oh, you’re so lucky. Now you have to do this thing that is exhausting.” I was embarrassed to ask, but Judd brought the cash like he said he would, and I got to talk to him a little bit about his relationship with money.

Everyone has different, strong feelings about money. I’ve been in Debtors Anonymous for years, and that’s fascinating, because you’ll meet people who are millionaires, yet they’re filled with this feeling of lack, or panic. Like, “I can’t make my mortgage in the Pacific Palisades, but I can’t possibly take my kids out of this extremely expensive private school.” I think they’ve done research on this: if you’re living below the poverty line, you are often much more generous dollar per dollar than someone who has billions.

I feel I could be more generous. I have a bit of an obsession with giving away eleven per cent of my income, and I can never figure out how to calculate that. This year I was, like, “It should be from the gross. It should be from before taxes.” Now I’m, like, “But I think that’s too much!” Part of this is O.C.D.

I wonder if that self-doubt is also part of the reason people don’t give any of their money away. It’s almost easier to shut out the question of giving entirely than to make the difficult moral decision of, Exactly how much can I afford to give?

The hilarious part is, twenty years ago, I gave what was, for me, a giant chunk of money—I think it was fifty thousand dollars—to a charity I believe in. I got a call saying, “Would you like to come in and have lunch with us?” And I’m, like, “No! No, I don’t want to do that.” That’s my own fear of intimacy, of getting involved in a community. I never want to be a part of anything, because once you become a part of something, you realize how imperfect it is. Anyway, I’m not sure if I’m answering anything properly.

This is great. So, after you texted Judd asking about payment for the documentary, he was gracious and said, “O.K., I’ll bring it”?

Well, first he said, “I can’t pay you, because if it’s a documentary, I’m not supposed to pay you.” And then I looked it up and saw that it’s actually being addressed by the Screen Actors Guild, because there are so many more documentaries about entertainers. At least, that’s what I remember reading. I am not a fact checker.

Also I thought, This isn’t going to be a documentary. Every time I’ve been in a documentary related to show business, there’s story creation. Like, “The Comedians of Comedy” was one of the best things I’ve been a part of. But they edited it to look like I was hanging out with all the guys. That was not true. I didn’t know those guys that well, and was completely freaked out by human interaction. I was too shy. But they tried to make me look cool. Like, “Oh, Bamford’s hanging out,” and it’s, like, “Well, she’s by herself by the pool reading twelve-step literature.”

I remember the first time we shot something [for “Paralyzed by Hope”], my ex-husband Scott had a painting show downtown. The director, Neil Berkeley—who I adore, super nice—had us “do” the entrance to the art gallery several times.

So that’s the work of it. And I know it’s not hard work. I’ve cleaned houses. I’ve waited tables, poorly. That’s hard work.

When the directors started shooting, were there any conversations about how much creative control you would or wouldn’t have?

When did you see the film for the first time?

The première, at Sundance. I think they sent me a cut, but I didn’t watch it. I’ve already had the chance to tell my story a billion times, so it’s, like, if you wanna take a crack at it, all right.

Were there parts of this documentary that felt like “The Comedians of Comedy” in the sense that you were, like, “That’s an imposed story line”? I don’t mean in a bad way, but which parts, if any, felt more like reality TV to you?

I don’t know. What I kept pitching to them was, “This should be all about financial literacy and finding out what everybody’s making.” What Judd is making, what the cameraman is making, what the restaurant we’re shooting at illegally and not asking permission from is not making. . . . I find that super fascinating.

My parents passed away. They both died earlier than they had thought and left me with a pretty hefty sum. I could retire if I moved to Duluth, maybe. I could live pretty O.K., and definitely not tour as much as I tour now. But do I want to do that? No. I wanna be part of the capitalism, man. I wanna earn or burn. What’s that about? What is this false engine that’s also making a lot of creativity happen? I find that interesting. I don’t have any answers on it at all.

But [the directors] were not interested in doing that. They’re, like, Mm-hmm, no, not that. So, I think it just depends on what you’re interested in. I think Judd is interested in watching people do standup. A lot of the documentary is me doing comedy stuff, which is great.

I just shot something else, with a company called Blonde Medicine, and it’s all about open-book accounting. So I tell everyone what I’m being paid. I was paid three hundred and fifty thousand dollars for a Netflix special. It’s the most I’ve ever been paid for a Netflix special, and I was, like, “Oh, hey, how much is the opener being paid?” And they said, “Five hundred bucks.” And I’m, like, “Oh, well, O.K., I think I’m going to hand her five grand.” [Netflix did not respond to a request for comment.]

You’re making a standup special about open-book accounting?

It’s sort of an obsession, because it isn’t spoken about. At all.

One of the things that struck me in the documentary was related to money, and what people think should and shouldn’t be done with it. You do these one-on-one shows where you recruit people from the internet and pay them an hourly fee to watch you rehearse new material. There’s a sequence in the film where a bunch of other professional comedians express their shock at the fact that you’re paying your fans, rather than the other way around. I was curious what you made of their surprise.

I assume people who are huge stars—Conan O’Brien, Judd Apatow—have staff or writers who will go through the jokes with them or who are working on things with them, which is exactly what I’m doing, but it’s just sort of one-offs instead.

You must know from talking to people in your industry: Do people have editors? Do people have coaches?

I think everybody has something different. I know that the co-writer for Jim Gaffigan is his wife, so they write his material together. My friend Jackie Kashian was hired to write jokes for standup, for a comedian who didn’t have the time. I’m very self-protective, so it’s ideal for me to do it on my own. There is the risk, of course, that people won’t laugh. But even if they complain or say, “Oh, that sucked,” I can go [mock-pensively], “Yeah, man. Life is disappointing on the regular.”

You’ve said that the most important thing for you isn’t if someone else finds the joke funny, but if you do.

What are you looking for in the reactions of strangers during these one-on-one rehearsals? Or is the goal to hire an accountability partner?

It’s only accountability. The main thing is that I’m getting out the words in the way that I’m planning to say them, and rehearsing. I don’t have the ambition to rehearse stuff on my own, so I have a babysitter watch me go through the jokes.

What’s wonderful about it is people are not that impressed by fame. People are doing their laundry, they’re picking up their kids, they’re driving. They’re barely listening to me.

The risk that someone doesn’t laugh doesn’t feel devastating, because they’re just one person.

It’s also really meaningful to me, because I get to talk to people who I would never get to talk to. I don’t go out after shows, because I do talk about mental health and I get overwhelmed, if somebody after is, like, “Hey, I need to tell you, my dad just died of suicide, we decided to come to the show.” I just don’t want to give anybody that blank face of “Thanks for coming to the show!” I want to be there for somebody.

You’ve talked extensively about your struggles with obsessive-compulsive disorder. And in the documentary, and in your memoir, you describe never having heard the term O.C.D. until you Googled “unwanted thoughts” when you were thirty-five?

That’s so shocking—no one before then had been, like, “That might be O.C.D.”?

No. I went to a therapist in Glendale who was just a regs therapist, and I tried to tell her about my fears, my intrusive thoughts, and she was, like, “I’m a mandated reporter.”

Oh, that was the therapist who called 911 on you. [The therapist was not familiar with O.C.D. and mistook Bamford’s obsessive fear about committing an act of violence as a threat.]

Yeah. So then I didn’t go for a couple more years. I got a book called “The Imp of the Mind.” Then I looked online, and saw the International OCD Foundation, which was great for me because I’d felt so alone as a kid. It was, like, I have this weird thing that I can’t tell anybody about. And then, once I told a professional O.C.D. person, they were, like [mimes looking up from a notepad], “Uh-huh. [In a bored voice.] So you wanna rape and kill people?” [Yawns dramatically.]

The therapist assigned you this cognitive-behavioral-therapy technique called “flooding.” The practice involves writing down your intrusive thoughts and fears, and even recording yourself talking about them, and playing that recording back to yourself. The idea is that by exposing yourself to these anxieties, you begin to rob them of their power. I wondered, while hearing you describe this: Isn’t that also what standup is?

It totally is. The thing becomes kind of boring and has lost all meaning to it. It’s totally therapeutic.

Saying the shameful thing out loud is a form of exposure therapy for you.

I heard a joke that really made me feel better, where the comedian said, “You know, sometimes I look at my dog’s butt and it’s kinda sexy.” [Laughs.] And I was, like, “Oh, that’s such a creepy thought, and thank you for sharing.” But I can only say those jokes in front of people who are fans or into mental health. I will tank everywhere else. I will bomb right in my neighborhood; I just did at a karaoke night. There are plenty of people who don’t want to hear the kind of material that I’m doing, or think it’s creepy, or weird, or gross, or boring, or tedious, or humorless.

You have permission in standup comedy to talk about things that you might not be able to talk about in other settings.

There are comics out there who are currently touring who have been accused of sexual assault, and nothing is really being done, especially by the comedians themselves. Like, they don’t say, “Hey, I actually went through a program. Here’s what I’m doing as a living amends by giving a million dollars to each of these people whose life I affected.” There’s none of that. I feel sad about the comedy community, because these guys are still working clubs, and no one’s protected who’s working there, at least as far as I can tell.

I think comedy is where you could at least talk about it. It’s where you could say, “Hey, there’s obviously a huge problem with people being isolated with these sexual compulsions.” These comedians who are so good at expressing specific experiences, isn’t there a way they could bring that experience to the world? Like, it’s the most taboo topic ever, and no one wants to hear about it.  Let’s not isolate these people. Let’s find out what’s going on in their minds.

I’ve been an addict—I was bulimic—I know the secretive behaviors. I’ve eaten all the food in somebody else’s refrigerator. I understand doing shameful things, and having those things revealed is a bummer, but it could also be a gift.

You share personal experiences onstage that are painful or embarrassing, but I’m assuming that at the time those things are happening to you, you’re not, like, “This could be great material.” Is the ability to turn something into a joke in a set a sign that you’ve processed the experience?

I would hope so. Some things I’m still not sure about even as I record them. I’m, like, should I? But I’ve read “The Artist’s Way.” You have no control over what blossoms.

I haven’t read it. I have it in my house, though.

Of course you do! Everybody has it. It was life-changing because it was, like, Oh, I could say, “I’m a comedian.” It helped me define that for myself. But some of it is very white privilege-y, very much, like [in a fancy woman’s voice], “Oh, my gosh, I just, I thought of blueberries, and then there was a bowl of blueberries.” It’s like, O.K., are you a fucking rich white lady living in the Hollywood Hills? The odds are pretty good there’s going to be a bowl of blueberries near you.

You recently got divorced. In an interview you did at the start of this year, you said you didn’t know how to talk or joke about your divorce yet.

A few weeks ago, I went to your show, where you brought up your divorce. I was wondering how it felt to start talking about it.

It feels O.K. now. We got very clear in our divorce decree that I will not talk about our divorce onstage beyond the facts of it. I have this verbal platform, and he’s a painter. He doesn’t really have a bunch of people he can spout off to. That felt fair. Also, there are ways to talk about it that don’t involve my ex, but that are about the universal.

The things that were untenable with us were that our views of the world were very different. The joke is, I grew up in Minnesota, so I see everyone as a new friend. Like, “Oh, noooo, we gotta let these two cars into our lane because they might be families of guinea pigs trying to go to a museum!” And he’s from Philadelphia, super tough, had a rough childhood, so for him, everything is incoming. Everything is death asking him to play chess.

He’s not wrong, and I’m not wrong. But it’s just a measure of whether you can live with each other. That kind of came to a head when the fires happened. We had a joyful divorce, and we have a good relationship sharing the doggies.

The documentary is a time capsule, in a way, because it shows the two of you together in your old house in Altadena, before the fires of 2025. What’s it been like to move here and live alone?

It’s been lovely. I have a friend who lives on the other side of this complex, and she’s awesome. All my neighbors are super friendly. Almost everybody’s Spanish-speaking, so I can practice my Spanish a little bit.

We owned a very beautiful house. It’s still there, in Altadena. But it was too isolated. I don’t like suburbs, it turns out. I don’t like that much house upkeep. I loved having people over. I love parties. But in this neighborhood all I have to do is rent a party place. There’s this co-working place that used to be a church, and that’s where I did a singles event the other week.

One hundred and fifty people showed up. A third were men, which was really impressive.

Two of the guys were shaking when they came. It was really adorable. I was proud of everybody who came, and I threw a chunk of change at it, so I think the next time I would want to see if it could support itself and have it truly be a community potluck.

Yes. Two couples left together. Nobody asked me out, which is hilarious. I wasn’t sure what to do about that. My sister was, like, “Maybe it seemed like you were too intimidating.” And I’m, like, “That seems like a punishing point of view. I’m just wandering around, tremoring, just like anyone.”

I’ve gone on a lot of internet dates, which are wonderful, too, but also can be very hopeless. I had a date with one guy, perfectly lovely. It was da, da, da, da, boop, da, da, da, boop, boop, boop, beep. Then he said, “So, I saw your comedy. You’re crazy!” It reminded me of somebody introducing me in the nineties: “This lady, she’s schizophrenic!” No, I’m a middle-of-the-road comedian who does voices. That’s all that’s happening here.

You’ve said that you’ve always been performing for your parents on some level. Your father, who appears in parts of the documentary, passed away in 2023, a few years after your mom. I was curious how their deaths have changed your relationship to your imagined audience.

My dad would watch me do whatever I wanted. I would practice doing speeches for him when I was a kid.

I was just listening to NPR, where Terry Gross was interviewing Yiyun Li, the writer whose two sons died of suicide. She was saying there’s a difference between sadness and depression. I think I feel that about my parents. I’ll never stop feeling sad that they’re not here. I shouldn’t compare myself to the lady losing her two sons. But it really spoke to me about how you never get over grief, but can still enjoy life.

In some ways, I know my parents didn’t always like my material, so they weren’t perfect at being the best audience or the most supportive. My mom had actually told me that when your parents die, there is some kind of freedom. When she was alive, my mom was always, like [imitates Marilyn Bamford]: “Honey, put on some lipstick. What’s going on with your hair? Are you not getting it done?” I don’t have to think about that anymore. That my mom might be disappointed in me in that way, or that my dad thinks I’m lazy.

I need to ask you about the vibrator bit with your dad. Right after your mom died, your dad tried to give your mom’s vibrators away to you and your sister, as though he was passing down a sacred family heirloom.

Do you feel like he knew how funny he was being when he handed his daughters a shoebox and said, “Your mom would’ve wanted you to have these”?

Oh, no. I think my dad, especially during that moment, was very sincere. I did kind of want to take them, but then my sister put them in the garbage. I was, like, “Well, that’s going to be fun for someone.” I still feel guilty for using that as a joke.

Your dad happily talks about it in the documentary, though. The filmmakers interview him, and he reiterates, “I really thought it was something that I could share with the two of you.”

[In the voice of her father, Joel Bamford.] “These things are expensive!”

My sister seemed slightly grossed out, but I thought it was very thoughtful. ♦

The doxing of Jane Doe and the fallout from the “Cornell Seven” rape case.

“She Made Me Do It”: a famed equestrian instructor shot his most annoying student.

Parents disagreed on whether to vaccinate their children. Then the kids got sick.
